Differences from the Standard Ebooks edition

566 places where se-llm differs from SE, with 670 separate changes; places with exactly the same difference are shown once, with the others folded under it: 456 in the words and 214 in the markup only. Each place shows the same passage three ways as source, with markup and line breaks: the Gutenberg HTML on top, the SE XHTML in the middle and the se-llm XHTML at the bottom. SE and se-llm are highlighted character by character where they differ, tags included; a red bar marks where one side has nothing. In Gutenberg the highlight is the passage behind a text change. Next to each place, every change has its own cell: what differs, the se-llm step that made se-llm's version, and a verdict. Tags are compared by element and by epub:type, xml:lang, class and role; ids and link targets are ignored. Verdicts are a model's judgment (Claude Sonnet) against the SE Manual of Style and se-llm's keep-the-Gutenberg-reading policy; markup changes with the same tags share one verdict. They are not proof.

markup
wordsBang! Bang!
SE<i>…</i>
se-llm<strong>…</strong>
se-llm step: [Semantic] Apply caps-and-bold (1 change)
SE correct semantic italics
Gutenberg only sets the opening words in capitals, which is a typographic lead-in. SE drops the caps, and <strong> is not valid for that (semantics section: no bold for style), so se-llm's <strong> on only the first 'Bang!' is wrong. SE's italics for the onomatopoeic sound is a reasonable choice.

SE correct abbreviation
The manual marks name titles such as 'St.' with <abbr epub:type="z3998:name-title">. se-llm left a bare <abbr> and so omitted the semantic. Gutenberg has plain 'St.'.

<p>When Frycollin ventured out of his cabin and saw all this water beneath him, fear took possession of him.</p>
<p>Beneath him is hardly the correct phrase; we should have said around him, for to an observer above the earth the abyss seems to curve up like a bowl to the horizon on a level with his eye, and the edge of the horizon seems to travel away as he moves towards it.</p>
<p>It is probable that Frycollin could not explain this effect physically, but he felt it all the same, and it produced on him that horror of the abyss from which certain people, brave though they may be, cannot free themselves. The Negro was wise enough to say nothing. With eyes shut he felt his way back to his cabin, resolving to stay there for some considerable time.</p>

<p>On the morning of the 15th the <i epub:type="se:name.vessel">Albatross</i> passed between the islands of Ascension and <abbr epub:type="z3998:name-title">St.</abbr> Helena, approaching the last, whose higher ground showed above the horizon for some hours. In the evenings of the 16th and 17th of July there was a curious phenomenon at sunset. In a higher latitude it might have been taken for the aurora borealis. The sun as it disappeared shot forth a number of multicolored rays, some of which were of a vivid green.</p>
<p>Was this due to cosmic dust which the earth was then passing through, and which reflected the last beams of the day? Some observers have assigned such an origin to these crepuscular lights, but their explanation would not have been maintained for long had they been on board the aeronef, where it was found that there were floating in the air tiny crystals of pyroxene, and glassy globes and slender needles of magnetic iron, analogous to the matter ejected by certain volcanoes. There could be no doubt that a volcanic eruption had projected this cloud into the air, where it was held in suspension while it drifted over the Atlantic.</p>
<p>And other phenomena were observed. On several occasions the sky took a strange grayish tint, and when the curtain of vapor was passed its surface seemed ridged with white feathery clouds and dotted with solid spangles, giving an appearance which could only be explained as due to a form of hail.</p>
<p>In the night between the 17th and 18th there appeared a greenish lunar bow, owing to the aeronef being between the full moon and a curtain of fine rain which went off into vapor before it reached the sea.</p>
<p>Did these phenomena portend a change in the weather? Perhaps. Anyhow, the wind, which had blown from the southwest since their leaving the coast of Africa, had now dropped to a calm. And in this tropical zone it was extremely cold, for Robur had risen to get the benefit of the higher air-currents, and taken steps to screen himself from the direct rays of the sun, which would have been almost insupportable.</p>
<p>In the southern hemisphere the month of July answers to the month of January in the northern, that is to say, it is the depth of winter, and if the <i epub:type="se:name.vessel">Albatross</i> was kept on her southerly course she would soon feel the effect of this.</p>

Lint resolution

Result: resolved. Owner and checker model: claude-opus-5-5. Repair rounds: 2. Owner calls: 3; checker calls: 3.

What the independent checker rejected:

  • The proposal accepts every occurrence, including chapter-14.xhtml line 67 '<p>P. and'. The proposal's own rationale says this 'P.' is a name initial. That is a markup omission, not a source punctuation reading. The t-029 hint requires an <abbr> element, and the book already marks initials as <abbr epub:type="z3998:given-name">W.</abbr> and <abbr epub:type="z3998:given-name">T.</abbr>. That occurrence needs a FIX that wraps it as <abbr epub:type="z3998:given-name">P.</abbr>, with the text left unchanged. It must not be accepted as is.
